Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Like, if I go back and check the pill organizer or the stove, or look over my lesson plans again, I usually feel a little calmer afterward.\n\nAnd if I can’t check it directly, I’ll kind of replay what I did in my head until it feels… settled, I guess. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it does help me move on—though sometimes it makes me run late. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals in a specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… not like a big formal ritual, I don’t think, but I do have to go over the same things a few times before I can feel okay leaving.\n\nLike before I leave the house or my classroom, I’ll check the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans… and if I can’t physically check, I’ll replay what I did in my head until it feels calm enough to move on. It’s a little embarrassing, because I know it’s probably more than I need to do, and sometimes it does make me run late. But it’s usually just a few things, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Mostly it’s that kind of thing—worrying I forgot something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong, or with my classroom, that I didn’t set up materials safely.

I mean, I usually know I probably handled it fine, but the doubt just pops back in anyway. It’s not like I’m worrying about everything all day, I don’t think… it’s more those “what if I missed something important?” thoughts. Is that helpful?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these repeated thoughts that I might’ve forgotten something important. It’s probably not a huge thing, but like… I’ll worry I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn’t set up my classroom materials safely enough. Even when I’m pretty sure I handled it, the doubt just kind of comes back and nags at me. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to get rid of these nagging thoughts?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I try not to make it a big thing, but before I leave the house or my classroom I’ll sometimes go back over things a few times—like checking the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd if I can’t physically check, I’ll sort of replay in my head what I did, just to make sure I didn’t miss anything. Usually once I feel calm enough, I can move on… though it does make me late sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not having these specific thoughts about forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’m just anxious all the time, if that’s what you mean. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, right? But I mostly notice it when one of those thoughts pops up. Otherwise I think I’m usually okay. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or anxious for no particular reason?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get tense like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m nervous for no reason most of the time. It usually feels tied to something specific… unless I’m just not noticing it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things over and over helps to reduce your anxiety about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, at least in the moment. Like, if I go back and check the pill organizer or the stove, or look over my lesson plans again, I usually feel a little calmer afterward.\n\nAnd if I can’t check it directly, I’ll kind of replay what I did in my head until it feels… settled, I guess. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it does help me move on—though sometimes it makes me run late. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals in a specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… not like a big formal ritual, I don’t think, but I do have to go over the same things a few times before I can feel okay leaving.\n\nLike before I leave the house or my classroom, I’ll check the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans… and if I can’t physically check, I’ll replay what I did in my head until it feels calm enough to move on. Usually once I feel calm enough, I can move on… though it does make me late sometimes. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not having these specific thoughts about forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think there’s anything else major, no. It’s really those nagging thoughts about maybe forgetting something important.

Not usually, no. I mean, I don’t think I just get anxious out of nowhere. When I notice it, there’s usually some thought behind it, even if it takes me a second to realize what it is.

Otherwise I think I’m usually okay. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or anxious for no particular reason?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get tense like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m nervous for no reason most of the time. It usually feels tied to something specific… unless I’m just not noticing it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things over and over helps to reduce your anxiety about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, at least in the moment. Like, if I go back and check the pill organizer or the stove, or look over my lesson plans again, I usually feel a little calmer afterward.\n\nAnd if I can’t check it directly, I’ll kind of replay what I did in my head until it feels… settled, I guess. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it does help me move on—though sometimes it makes me run late. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals in a specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… not like a big formal ritual, I don’t think, but I do have to go over the same things a few times before I can feel okay leaving.\n\nLike before I leave the house or my classroom, I’ll check the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans… and if I can’t physically check, I’ll replay what I did in my head until it feels calm enough to move on. It’s a little embarrassing, because I know it’s probably more than I need to do, and sometimes it does make me run late. But it’s usually just a few things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. Not panic, exactly, but I’d feel really unsettled.\n\nIf I couldn’t go back over the pill organizer or the stove, or look again at my lesson plans before leaving, I’d probably keep replaying it in my head anyway until I felt calm enough. I know it’s not the most practical thing, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but without doing it I think it would bother me and be hard to just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other things in your life besides possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that kind of thing—worrying I forgot something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong, or with my classroom, that I didn’t set up materials safely.\n\nI mean, I usually know I probably handled it fine, but the doubt just pops back in anyway. It’s not like I’m worrying about everything all day, I don’t think… it’s more those “what if I missed something important?” thoughts. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious even when you're not thinking about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nMost of the time when I feel tense, it’s tied to that specific thought that I might’ve missed something or left something undone. If I’m busy teaching or doing something unrelated, I’m usually okay. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m just anxious for no reason all the time. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the thoughts of possibly forgetting something, or does it also occur in other situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly just about that, I think—the possibility that I forgot something important.\n\nLike, I’ll leave and then this thought comes up that maybe I set my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or maybe I didn’t make the classroom materials safe enough, even though I can usually tell myself I probably did it fine. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety ever triggered by something other than these thoughts of possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… it really seems to come back to that same kind of thought, that I might’ve forgotten something important.\n\nLike I said, it’ll be something like worrying I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn’t set up my classroom materials safely enough. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals in a specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… not like a big formal ritual, I don’t think, but I do have to go over the same things a few times before I can feel okay leaving.\n\nLike before I leave the house or my classroom, I’ll check the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans… and if I can’t physically check, I’ll replay what I did in my head until it feels calm enough to move on. It’s a little embarrassing, because I know it’s probably more than I need to do, and sometimes it does make me run late. But it’s usually just a few things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. Not panic, exactly, but I’d feel really unsettled.\n\nIf I couldn’t go back over the pill organizer or the stove, or look again at my lesson plans before leaving, I’d probably keep replaying it in my head anyway until I felt calm enough. I know it’s not the most practical thing, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but without doing it I think it would bother me and be hard to just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other things in your life besides possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that kind of thing—worrying I forgot something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong, or with my classroom, that I didn’t set up materials safely.\n\nI mean, I usually know I probably handled it fine, but the doubt just pops back in anyway. It’s not like I’m worrying about everything all day, I don’t think… it’s more those “what if I missed something important?” thoughts. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious even when you're not thinking about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nMost of the time when I feel tense, it’s tied to that specific thought that I might’ve missed something or left something undone. If I’m busy teaching or doing something unrelated, I’m usually okay. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m just anxious for no reason all the time. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the thoughts of possibly forgetting something, or does it also occur in other situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly just about that, I think—the possibility that I forgot something important.\n\nLike, I’ll leave and then this thought comes up that maybe I set my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or maybe I didn’t make the classroom materials safe enough, even though I can usually tell myself I probably did it fine. The doubt just kind of comes back anyway.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it… I’m not anxious in every situation. It’s more those specific “what if I missed something?” thoughts. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it doesn't have anything to do with possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, I don’t think so. When I came in, it was mainly because of those nagging thoughts that I forgot something important—like my aunt’s medicine, or whether I set up my classroom materials safely.\n\nEven when part of me knows I probably did it right, the doubt just keeps popping back in. But if it’s not connected to that kind of “what if I forgot?” thought, I usually don’t feel anxious in the same way. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety ever triggered by something other than these thoughts of possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… it really seems to come back to that same kind of thought, that I might’ve forgotten something important.\n\nLike I said, it’ll be something like worrying I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn’t set up my classroom materials safely enough. Even when I can tell myself I probably handled it, the doubt sort of pops back in anyway.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say it’s triggered by lots of other things, at least not that I’ve noticed. Is that clear?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides these thoughts of possibly forgetting something, that you worry about or fear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything else major, no. It’s really those nagging thoughts about maybe forgetting something important.\n\nLike… I’ll worry I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn’t set up my classroom materials safely enough. And even when I know, logically, I probably handled it, the doubt just pops back into my head again.\n\nI hope that answers it—I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s the main thing I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling anxious without any specific reason or thought triggering it?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I don’t think I just get anxious out of nowhere. When I notice it, there’s usually some thought behind it, even if it takes me a second to realize what it is.\n\nBut if you mean like just suddenly feeling anxious for no reason at all… I haven’t really noticed that happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that makes you feel anxious besides the thought of forgetting something important?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least nothing that stands out to me the way that does. It’s mostly that same worry that I forgot something important, like with my aunt’s medicine or things in my classroom.\n\nI know it probably sounds small, and I can usually tell myself I most likely handled it, but the doubt still kind of comes back. But besides that specific kind of thought, I don’t think there’s another big thing making me anxious. Is that okay—does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it ever the case that you feel anxious without any thought or worry about forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, not that I’ve noticed. When I feel anxious, it usually does seem tied to that same kind of thought, like, “Did I forget something important?” or “Did I handle that the right way?”\n\nSometimes it takes me a minute to realize that’s what I’m worrying about, but it usually comes back to things like my aunt’s medicine or something at school. I’m not really having anxiety that just appears with no thought behind it, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals in a specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… not like a big formal ritual, I don’t think, but I do have to go over the same things a few times before I can feel okay leaving.\n\nLike before I leave the house or my classroom, I’ll check the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans… and if I can’t physically check, I’ll replay what I did in my head until it feels calm enough to move on. It’s a little embarrassing, because I know it’s probably more than I need to do, and sometimes it does make me run late. But it’s usually just a few things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. Not panic, exactly, but I’d feel really unsettled.\n\nIf I couldn’t go back over the pill organizer or the stove, or look again at my lesson plans before leaving, I’d probably keep replaying it in my head anyway until I felt calm enough. I know it’s not the most practical thing, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but without doing it I think it would bother me and be hard to just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other things in your life besides possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that kind of thing—worrying I forgot something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong, or with my classroom, that I didn’t set up materials safely.\n\nI mean, I usually know I probably handled it fine, but the doubt just pops back in anyway. It’s not like I’m worrying about everything all day, I don’t think… it’s more those “what if I missed something important?” thoughts. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious even when you're not thinking about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nMost of the time when I feel tense, it’s tied to that specific thought that I might’ve missed something or left something undone. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the thoughts of possibly forgetting something, or does it also occur in other situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly just about that, I think—the possibility that I forgot something important.\n\nLike, I’ll leave and then this thought comes up that maybe I set my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or maybe I didn’t make the classroom materials safe enough, even though I can usually tell myself I probably did it fine. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge even when you're not having these specific thoughts about forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’m just anxious all the time, if that’s what you mean. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, right? But I mostly notice it when one of those thoughts pops up. Otherwise I think I’m usually okay. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or anxious for no particular reason?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get tense like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m nervous for no reason most of the time. It usually feels tied to something specific… unless I’m just not noticing it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things over and over helps to reduce your anxiety about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, at least in the moment. Like, if I go back and check the pill organizer or the stove, or look over my lesson plans again, I usually feel a little calmer afterward.\n\nAnd if I can’t check it directly, I’ll kind of replay what I did in my head until it feels… settled, I guess. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it does help me move on—though sometimes it makes me run late. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking rituals in a specific way to feel calm?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes… not like a big formal ritual, I don’t think, but I do have to go over the same things a few times before I can feel okay leaving.\n\nLike before I leave the house or my classroom, I’ll check the pill organizer, the stove, my lesson plans… and if I can’t physically check, I’ll replay what I did in my head until it feels calm enough to move on. It’s a little embarrassing, because I know it’s probably more than I need to do, and sometimes it does make me run late. But it’s usually just a few things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you couldn't perform your checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. Not panic, exactly, but I’d feel really unsettled.\n\nIf I couldn’t go back over the pill organizer or the stove, or look again at my lesson plans before leaving, I’d probably keep replaying it in my head anyway until I felt calm enough. I know it’s not the most practical thing, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but without doing it I think it would bother me and be hard to just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other things in your life besides possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that kind of thing—worrying I forgot something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong, or with my classroom, that I didn’t set up materials safely.\n\nI mean, I usually know I probably handled it fine, but the doubt just pops back in anyway. It’s not like I’m worrying about everything all day, I don’t think… it’s more those “what if I missed something important?” thoughts. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're anxious even when you're not thinking about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t really noticed it that way.\n\nMost of the time when I feel tense, it’s tied to that specific thought that I might’ve missed something or left something undone. If I’m busy teaching or doing something unrelated, I’m usually okay. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m just anxious for no reason all the time. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety only about the thoughts of possibly forgetting something, or does it also occur in other situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly just about that, I think—the possibility that I forgot something important.\n\nLike, I’ll leave and then this thought comes up that maybe I set my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or maybe I didn’t make the classroom materials safe enough, even though I can usually tell myself I probably did it fine. The doubt just kind of comes back anyway.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it… I’m not anxious in every situation. It’s more those specific “what if I missed something?” thoughts. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it doesn't have anything to do with possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, I don’t think so. When I came in, it was mainly because of those nagging thoughts that I forgot something important—like my aunt’s medicine, or whether I set up my classroom materials safely.\n\nEven when part of me knows I probably did it right, the doubt just keeps popping back in. But if it’s not connected to that kind of “what if I forgot?” thought, I usually don’t feel anxious in the same way. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety ever triggered by something other than these thoughts of possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… it really seems to come back to that same kind of thought, that I might’ve forgotten something important.\n\nLike I said, it’ll be something like worrying I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn’t set up my classroom materials safely enough. Even when I can tell myself I probably handled it, the doubt sort of pops back in anyway.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say it’s triggered by lots of other things, at least not that I’ve noticed. Is that clear?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides these thoughts of possibly forgetting something, that you worry about or fear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything else major, no. It’s really those nagging thoughts about maybe forgetting something important.\n\nLike… I’ll worry I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or that I didn’t set up my classroom materials safely enough. And even when I know, logically, I probably handled it, the doubt just pops back into my head again.\n\nI hope that answers it—I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but that’s the main thing I’m afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling anxious without any specific reason or thought triggering it?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. I mean, I don’t think I just get anxious out of nowhere. When I notice it, there’s usually some thought behind it, even if it takes me a second to realize what it is.\n\nBut if you mean like just suddenly feeling anxious for no reason at all… I haven’t really noticed that happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that makes you feel anxious besides the thought of forgetting something important?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least nothing that stands out to me the way that does. It’s mostly that same worry that I forgot something important, like with my aunt’s medicine or things in my classroom.\n\nI know it probably sounds small, and I can usually tell myself I most likely handled it, but the doubt still kind of comes back. But besides that specific kind of thought, I don’t think there’s another big thing making me anxious. Is that okay—does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is it ever the case that you feel anxious without any thought or worry about forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, not that I’ve noticed. When I feel anxious, it usually does seem tied to that same kind of thought, like, “Did I forget something important?” or “Did I handle that the right way?”\n\nSometimes it takes me a minute to realize that’s what I’m worrying about, but it usually comes back to things like my aunt’s medicine or something at school. I’m not really having anxiety that just appears with no thought behind it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a situation where you feel anxious and you're not thinking about forgetting something at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. But I don’t really notice anxiety that isn’t tied to that kind of worry, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall any recent situation where you felt anxious without thinking about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, but… no, not really. The recent times I can remember feeling anxious were pretty much all about that same nagging worry that I forgot something important.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, worrying I left it out wrong, or at school wondering if I set up materials safely. Even when I know I probably did handle it, the doubt just pops back in. I don’t think I’ve had a recent anxious moment where that wasn’t part of it, if that answers it okay.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the anxiety I came in about is pretty much those nagging “what if I forgot something important” thoughts.

Like with my aunt’s medicine or whether I set things up safely in my classroom… even when I know I probably handled it, the doubt keeps popping back into my head. So I can’t really think of a time lately where it felt completely unrelated to that. Does that make sense?
